```
Now I have a thorough understanding of the paper's content, experimental evidence, strengths, and weaknesses. Let me deliver the assessment.

---

## Brutally Honest Assessment: What Would Push This Paper Above 80% Acceptance

### What Category of Contribution Is This?

This paper falls in the **analysis/understanding + method** category, not the method-first category. The core value is the diagnosis (normalization breaks MoE routing), not the fix (route on raw input). This positioning is critical because it determines what reviewers will evaluate against.

The papers that defined the time series foundation model space (PatchTST, MOMENT, DLinear, Moirai) were all **paradigm-setters**: they introduced a new way of thinking about the problem. PatchTST proved patching works; DLinear proved transformers are overkill on LTSF; MOMENT proved pretraining transfers. Each changed how practitioners think. An 8-paper at NeurIPS in this space needs to either set a new paradigm or deliver an insight so deep that it restructures how the community approaches an existing one.

Your paper is attempting the latter: "normalization and MoE routing are fundamentally incompatible." The question is whether the community believes this insight is deep enough to warrant acceptance.

---

### Structural Limitations That Cap Acceptance Probability

**Limitation 1: The fix is trivially obvious once the diagnosis is stated.**

This is the single largest structural risk, and no amount of experiments can fully resolve it. The diagnosis is: "RevIN strips mean and scale; the router needs mean and scale; therefore route before RevIN." The fix is: `route(raw_x)`. A reviewer can reconstruct the entire paper from a one-sentence summary.

The paper already tries to address this by positioning the diagnosis as the contribution and arguing the fix's simplicity proves the diagnosis's precision. This is a valid argument, but it has a ceiling. The diagnosis itself, while carefully validated, is not especially surprising. Anyone who understands what RevIN does (it removes mean and scale) and what MoE routers do (they differentiate between inputs) could predict this failure in hindsight. The 720-run rescue sweep and the Timer-XL contrast are impressive evidence that this is the *right* diagnosis, but they do not make the diagnosis *deeper*.

Compare to DLinear's contribution: "Transformers are overkill for LTSF." That paper's fix was also trivially simple (a linear model), but the insight was genuinely shocking to the community and forced a paradigm rethink. Your insight is narrower: "MoE routing breaks under normalization" applies only when someone tries to add MoE adapters to normalized TSFMs, which is not yet a common practice.

**Limitation 2: The DLinear gap undermines the practical case.**

The paper is honest about this, which is good. But the numbers are stark: on the primary backbone (MOMENT-small), frozen RR-MoA gets 0.680 on ETTh1 where DLinear gets 0.416. That is a 63% gap. The Residual-IA+ work partially addresses this (108/123 match-or-beat across backbones), but on MOMENT-small at H=96 it is only 5/6 match-or-beat, and ETTh1 still shows a +2.2% gap. More importantly, the Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix L) honestly shows the TSFM contributes essentially nothing on 3/6 datasets. A reviewer can reasonably ask: "Why use a 90M-parameter TSFM if a 449K-parameter MLP ensemble matches it?"

The deployment argument (multi-tenant serving, hot-swappable adapters) is real but does not appear in the experimental evaluation. There is no latency-vs-accuracy Pareto curve, no multi-tenant simulation, no real-world deployment case study. The deployment motivation remains entirely in prose.

**Limitation 3: Scope and generality.**

The phenomenon (routing collapse under instance normalization) is specific to a particular combination of design choices: frozen backbone + RevIN + MoE adapter. The vision control experiment shows the opposite effect (normalization prevents collapse in vision). This is intellectually interesting but also limits the paper's generality claim. The principle does not transfer beyond TSFMs with instance normalization.

---

### Paths to 80%: Ranked by Feasibility and Impact

**Path 1 (HIGH IMPACT, MEDIUM FEASIBILITY, ~2 weeks): Demonstrate the principle on NLP/vision foundation models with normalization**

The single most impactful result would be to show that normalization-induced routing collapse occurs outside time series. If you can demonstrate that, say, a BERT-family model with LayerNorm (which normalizes per-feature, not per-instance) shows analogous routing degradation when MoE adapters are attached, and that raw-input routing fixes it, the paper's scope jumps from "time series niche" to "general principle for adapter MoE on any foundation model." The vision experiment already shows the co-adaptation mechanism is cross-modal. What you need is a positive example where normalization causes (not prevents) collapse in another modality.

Concrete experiment: Take a pre-trained ViT with CLS-token normalization or a language model with RMS-Norm. Add MoE adapters. Check if routing collapses. If yes, test raw-input routing as the fix. Even a single positive result would dramatically expand the paper's scope.

Feasibility: Moderate. Requires implementing the MoE adapter on a different backbone family. The existing codebase does not support this, so it is new engineering work, not a configuration change.

**Path 2 (HIGH IMPACT, LOW FEASIBILITY): Get Residual-IA+ to genuinely beat DLinear on MOMENT-small**

If Residual-IA+ achieved 6/6 match-or-beat DLinear on MOMENT-small at H=96 (not just 5/6), this would substantially weaken the "just use DLinear" attack. Currently ETTh1 is the holdout at +2.2%. The paper itself diagnoses this as a short-horizon memorization effect specific to the smallest dataset, and the result does reach 6/6 at H=192.

Feasibility: Low. You have already run 1,025+ diagnostic runs on this gap. The postmortem documents show the fundamental information bottleneck. Getting the last dataset will likely require either a better backbone or an architectural breakthrough in the raw branch, neither of which is a quick fix.

**Path 3 (MEDIUM IMPACT, HIGH FEASIBILITY, ~1 week): Reframe the paper as a general principle paper, not a method paper**

The paper currently tries to be three things: a diagnosis paper, a method paper (RR-MoA), and a gap-closing paper (Residual-IA+). This "triple threat" framing was noted as a risk in your own RESEARCH_STATUS.md. The gap-closing story (Residual-IA+) takes enormous appendix space and introduces complexity that dilutes the core message.

Consider cutting Residual-IA+ from the main text entirely. Move it to the appendix as a "practical extension." The main paper becomes: (1) We discover normalization-induced routing collapse. (2) We prove why via MI decomposition. (3) The minimal fix (route on raw) works perfectly (54/54 wins). (4) The theory predicts when it helps and when it does not (rho=-0.88). This is a clean, focused story that a reviewer can evaluate in 20 minutes. The Residual-IA+ story, while impressive, introduces three more failure modes (gate pathology, parameter bloat, NLinear) that create reviewer surface area without strengthening the core claim.

This reframing also sidesteps the DLinear gap: if the paper is explicitly about the diagnosis of a failure mode and its minimal fix, not about matching supervised baselines, the DLinear comparison becomes context rather than the paper's burden to resolve.

Feasibility: High. This is purely a writing change. No new experiments needed.

**Path 4 (MEDIUM IMPACT, MEDIUM FEASIBILITY, ~1 week): Tighten the theory**

The MI decomposition (Proposition 1) is currently described as "textbook-level; applying it to RevIN is the novelty." That is accurate but undersells the contribution. The non-obvious parts are: (a) the leakage term epsilon varies dramatically by dataset (0.09 to 1.26 nats), (b) H(E|S) does NOT correlate with improvement while R(D) does, and (c) the explanation for this (R(D) measures routing potential, not MI loss). These are genuinely interesting statistical observations.

To strengthen the theory, you could: (i) derive a tighter bound that accounts for the leakage structure, perhaps using the spectral properties of the RevIN operator; (ii) prove that R(D) is a sufficient statistic for predicting routing benefit under mild assumptions; (iii) provide a formal characterization of when the full-information corner (Part iii) holds approximately.

The footnote on line 475 noting that H(E|S) does not correlate with Delta% while R(D) does is currently buried. This should be a headline finding: you have empirical evidence that the mechanism of information loss is more predictive than the magnitude of information loss. That is a non-obvious theoretical insight.

Feasibility: Medium. Requires new theoretical work, which may or may not yield clean results.

**Path 5 (LOW-MEDIUM IMPACT, HIGH FEASIBILITY, ~3 days): Add a "when NOT to use RR-MoA" section to the main text**

The paper already has negative results (Traffic R=0.14, Chronos/Timer-XL negative controls), but they are scattered. A focused section that says "Compute R(D); if R < 0.2, do not use RR-MoA; use standard hidden-state routing instead" would be a strong practitioner contribution. Reviewers who score 8+ often cite "gives me actionable guidance" as a factor. The R(D) metric as a practitioner decision tool is undersold.

---

### Is 80% Structurally Reachable?

Yes, but barely, and only via Path 3 (reframing) combined with Path 1 or Path 4.

The paper's current form has structural tension between "deep diagnosis paper" and "competitive method paper." Deep diagnosis papers can score 8+ at NeurIPS when the diagnosis is surprising and the evidence is airtight. Your evidence is airtight (4,259 runs, 107 verification checks, seven converging causal controls). The question is whether the diagnosis is surprising enough. The Timer-XL contrast (opposite entropy trajectories under identical conditions) is genuinely striking evidence. The dose-response phase transition (entropy stays high while MSE degrades 283%) is a non-obvious finding that separates your story from the naive "RevIN kills routing" summary.

The paper at its best is a forensic analysis paper---like a detective story where the suspect (normalization) is identified through converging lines of evidence, the motive (MI destruction) is proven, and the case is so airtight that the remedy is obvious. That framing would score higher than the current framing, which tries to also be a method paper and a gap-closing paper.

**My estimate of paths to 80%:**

- Current paper as-is: 55-65% (the "triple threat" structure creates reviewer fatigue, DLinear gap is a persistent weakness)
- Path 3 alone (reframe as diagnosis paper): 65-72% (cleaner story, but scope concern remains)
- Path 3 + Path 5 (reframe + practitioner guideline): 68-75%
- Path 3 + Path 1 (reframe + cross-modal evidence): 75-82% (this is the most realistic path to 80%)
- Path 3 + Path 4 (reframe + tighter theory): 70-78% (only if the theory yields clean results)

**The "silver bullet" result:** If you could show normalization-induced routing collapse on a large language model (even a small one like TinyBERT or GPT-2), and that raw-input routing fixes it, the paper immediately becomes a general ML contribution rather than a time series niche contribution. That would push acceptance above 80% with high confidence. The existing vision experiment (ResNet-18 + CIFAR-10) shows the opposite effect (normalization prevents collapse), but that is because InstanceNorm in vision does not strip routing-relevant statistics. You need a case where it does. Consider models with aggressive input normalization (batch-normalized CNNs, models with input standardization layers).

---

### What Makes a 6 vs an 8 at NeurIPS in This Space

Papers that score 6 in the time series ML space typically: have solid experiments on standard benchmarks, propose a reasonable method, but leave reviewers feeling "this is incremental" or "this is a niche contribution." They often have good execution but lack a surprising core finding.

Papers that score 8 typically have one of: (a) a genuinely surprising finding that changes how practitioners think (DLinear), (b) a method that achieves new SOTA on a broad benchmark with clean ablations (PatchTST), or (c) a theoretical insight that explains a previously mysterious phenomenon with empirical validation.

Your paper is closest to (c). The normalization-routing incompatibility is a real phenomenon that nobody has documented before. The evidence is excellent. The remaining gap to 8 is: (i) the phenomenon is narrower than the framing suggests (only TSFMs with RevIN, not all normalized backbones), and (ii) the fix is too simple to carry a method contribution on its own.

The most effective strategy is to accept that the fix is simple, stop trying to dress it up with Residual-IA+ complexity, and instead double down on making the diagnosis as general and surprising as possible. Every reviewer minute spent reading about gate pathology and NLinear warmup is a minute not spent appreciating the Timer-XL entropy contrast or the dose-response phase transition.

### Critical Files for Implementation

- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex` -- the 2,113-line paper that needs structural reframing (consolidate the diagnosis, move Residual-IA+ to appendix)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_vision_moe_collapse_v2.py` -- the vision MoE control experiment; this is the template for extending the cross-modal validation (Path 1)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_adamix.py` -- the MoE rescue infrastructure; would need to be adapted for any new backbone family
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/model.py` -- backbone loading dispatch; would need new branches for any NLP/vision backbone experiments
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/analyze_routing_signal_ratio.py` -- the R(D) computation; strengthening this into a formal practitioner decision tool (Path 5) requires extending this script
```

Here's the deep analysis on reaching 80%+:

---

## Path to 80%+: Honest Assessment

### Why you're capped at ~68% right now

The paper is structurally a **diagnosis paper** trying to also be a **method paper** and a **gap-closing paper**. This triple-threat framing creates reviewer fatigue and surface area for attack. The fix (`route(raw_x)`) is a one-liner that anyone could reconstruct from a one-sentence summary of the diagnosis.

Papers that score **8+** at NeurIPS in the TS/FM space do one of:
- (a) A genuinely shocking finding that changes practice (DLinear)
- (b) New SOTA on a broad benchmark (PatchTST, Moirai)
- (c) A theoretical insight that explains a previously mysterious phenomenon

You're closest to (c), but the phenomenon is narrower than the framing suggests — only TSFMs with RevIN, not all normalized backbones.

### Ranked paths to 80%+

| Path | Impact | Feasibility | Target |
|------|--------|-------------|--------|
| **Reframe as pure diagnosis paper** (move Residual-IA+ to appendix, cut method complexity) | Medium | High (writing only) | 65-72% |
| **Cross-modal positive evidence** (show collapse on an NLP or vision model with input normalization) | Very High | Medium (~1-2 weeks) | **75-82%** |
| **Formal R(D) practitioner guideline** (compute R, if R<0.2 don't use MoE adapters) | Low-Medium | High (3 days) | +3-5% |
| **Tighter theory** (prove R(D) is sufficient statistic for routing benefit) | Medium | Medium (may not yield clean results) | 70-78% |

### The "silver bullet" to 80%+

**Path 3 (reframe) + Path 1 (cross-modal)** is the only realistic route to 80%.

If you can show normalization-induced routing collapse on **any non-TS foundation model** — even a small one like TinyBERT or a BatchNorm-heavy CNN — and raw-input routing fixes it, the paper immediately becomes a **general ML contribution** rather than a time series niche paper. The current vision experiment shows the *opposite* (normalization prevents collapse in ResNet+CIFAR-10), which is interesting but doesn't expand scope.

**What to look for:** Models where input normalization strips task-relevant routing features. Candidates:
- BatchNorm-frozen CNNs where BN removes batch-level statistics useful for routing
- RMS-Norm language models where normalization homogenizes token representations
- Any pretrained model with aggressive input standardization + frozen weights

### The structural reframe

Stop trying to make RR-MoA a competitive *method*. Accept the fix is simple. Double down on the *detective story*: normalization is the suspect, MI destruction is the motive, 7 converging lines of evidence prove the case. Every page spent on Residual-IA+ gate pathology is a page *not* spent on the Timer-XL entropy contrast or dose-response phase transition — which are the results that make reviewers say "I didn't know that."

**Bottom line:** 80%+ requires either expanding the scope (cross-modal evidence) or drastically sharpening the focus (pure diagnosis paper). Both are achievable in 2 weeks, but not simultaneously.

## COMPREHENSIVE EXPLORATION REPORT: Vision MoE Experiment & Cross-Modal ViT Requirements

### 1. VISION MOE EXPERIMENT FULL ANALYSIS

#### 1.1 Script Location & Structure
**File:** `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_vision_moe_collapse_v2.py` (467 lines total)
**Orchestration:** `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_vision_moe_race.sh` (29 lines)

#### 1.2 Architecture Deep Dive

**Backbone:** ResNet-18 adapted for CIFAR-10 (lines 42-69)
- Input: 32×32 RGB images (CIFAR-10)
- Adapted `conv1`: 3×3 kernel, stride=1, padding=1 (vs standard 7×7 stride=2) to avoid aggressive downsampling on small images
- Removed `maxpool` layer (replaced with `nn.Identity()`)
- Output: Feature maps (B, 512, 4, 4) — 512 channels, 4×4 spatial grid
- Pretrained on ImageNet weights (via `torchvision.models.ResNet18_Weights.DEFAULT`)
- Structure: conv1 → bn1 → relu → layer1 → layer2 → layer3 → layer4

**Expert Pool (K=5):** Architecturally diverse experts matching time-series RR-MoA (lines 76-134)

| Expert Type | Architecture | Input | Output | Code Location |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MeanPoolExpert | Global avg pool → Linear | (B,512,4,4) | (B,10) | lines 76-84 |
| MaxPoolExpert | Global max pool → Linear | (B,512,4,4) | (B,10) | lines 87-95 |
| AttentionPoolExpert | Learned spatial attention weights → weighted pool → Linear | (B,512,4,4) | (B,10) | lines 98-110 |
| ConvPoolExpert | 1×1 conv (512→128) + ReLU → avg pool → Linear | (B,512,4,4) | (B,10) | lines 113-123 |
| LastPatchExpert | Extract bottom-right patch features → Linear (analog of last-token in TS) | (B,512,4,4) | (B,10) | lines 126-134 |

**MoE Router (lines 141-201, class `VisionMoEv2`):**
- **Feature normalization** (before routing): Applied on spatial feature maps BEFORE expert execution
  - `instancenorm`: `nn.InstanceNorm2d(512, affine=False)` — strips per-channel spatial mean/variance
  - `batchnorm`: `nn.BatchNorm2d(512)` — batch-wise normalization with learnable affine
  - `none`: Identity (no normalization)
- **Router input**: Pooled normalized features via `AdaptiveAvgPool2d(1)` → flatten → (B, 512)
- **Routing gate**: Linear layer (512 → K=5) followed by softmax → (B, K) routing weights
- **Expert combination**: Weighted sum over all K=5 expert outputs using routing weights

#### 1.3 The 4 Experimental Conditions (lines 355-360)

| ID | Config | frozen | norm_type | Expected Outcome | Actual Result |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | Unfrozen + InstanceNorm2d | False | "instancenorm" | Collapse expected | **No collapse** (entropy 1.475±0.021) |
| B | Frozen + InstanceNorm2d | True | "instancenorm" | No collapse expected | No collapse (entropy 0.992±0.041) |
| C | Unfrozen + No norm | False | "none" | No collapse expected | **COLLAPSE** (entropy 0.184±0.260) ✓ |
| D | Unfrozen + BatchNorm2d | False | "batchnorm" | Collapse expected | **No collapse** (entropy 1.476±0.031) |

**KEY FINDING (lines 1372-1375 of main.tex):** Normalization **PREVENTS** collapse in vision, opposite to time series. See main.tex lines 1380-1382 explanation.

#### 1.4 Training & Tracking (lines 240-327)

**Training loop:**
- Freezing control (lines 245-252): Frozen backbone → `backbone.eval()` + grad disabled. Unfrozen → backbone.train(), params = backbone + MoE
- Optimizer: Adam (lr=1e-3 by default)
- Loss: CrossEntropyLoss (no load-balancing loss to allow natural collapse)
- No validation loop during training

**Per-step metrics tracked in trajectory (lines 314-326):**
```json
{
  "step": global_step,
  "epoch": epoch,
  "loss": train_CE_loss,
  "routing_entropy": entropy_scalar,
  "routing_max_weight": max(mean_routing_weights),
  "mean_routing_weights": [w_1, ..., w_5],  # mean across batch
  "expert_usage": [usage_1, ..., usage_5],  # fraction of samples where expert k has max weight
  "expert_grad_norms": [norm_1, ..., norm_5],
  "router_grad_norm": scalar,
  "backbone_grad_norm": scalar,
  "train_acc": batch_accuracy
}
```

**Collapse detection (line 426):** `collapsed = entropy_final < 0.3` (max entropy for K=5 is ln(5)≈1.609)

#### 1.5 Results & Output Format

**Per condition outputs (lines 430-449):**
- JSON trajectory file: `trajectory_{cond_name}_{seed}.jsonl` — one dict per line, one line per training step
- Summary JSON: `collapse_summary_{seed}.json` (lines 451-453) with:
  ```json
  {
    "seed": 42,
    "epochs": 20,
    "K": 5,
    "conditions": {
      "A_unfrozen_instancenorm": {
        "frozen": false,
        "norm_type": "instancenorm",
        "test_acc": 0.919,
        "test_entropy": 1.475,
        "entropy_step0": ...,
        "entropy_step50": ...,
        "entropy_step200": ...,
        "entropy_final": 1.475,
        "final_weights": [...],
        "final_usage": [...],
        "collapsed": false,
        "elapsed_sec": 45.3,
        "n_trainable_params": 12345
      },
      ...
    }
  }
  ```

| C | Unfrozen + No norm | **0.184 ± 0.260** | **Yes** | 0.921 |
| D | Unfrozen + BatchNorm2d | 1.476 ± 0.031 | No | 0.919 |

**Claims (lines 1356-1382):**
1. **Normalization-routing tension is modality-general** (line 1356): Mechanism confirmed across time-series and vision
2. **Key finding (line 1380)**: "normalization prevents rather than causes collapse in vision" — OPPOSITE of time-series (RevIN causes collapse; InstanceNorm2d prevents it)
3. **Proposition 2 validation (line 1382)**: In vision, routing-discriminative info resides in spatial patterns, NOT per-channel statistics. InstanceNorm2d strips per-channel spatial mean/variance (negligible routing info for image classification)
4. **Traffic analogy (line 1382)**: Vision MoE is like Traffic dataset (R=0.14, low routing signal) where normalization doesn't help

**Results reported:** 3 seeds, 20 epochs, mean±std over runs. Collapse threshold: entropy ≤ 0.3 (hardcoded at line 426)

### 5. WHAT'S NEEDED TO ADD A CROSS-MODAL VIT EXPERIMENT

#### 5.1 Missing Components
1. **Vision Transformer backbone**: Currently none in codebase
2. **ViT-to-image adapter logic**: ResNet outputs (B,512,4,4); ViT typically outputs (B, N_patches, hidden_dim) with cls token handling
3. **Timm or native ViT loading**: Could use `torchvision.models.vision_transformer.vit_b_16` (PyTorch 2.1.0+) but not currently imported
4. **Feature extraction from ViT**: ViT outputs sequence of patch embeddings + cls token; need to pool/extract similarly to ResNet
5. **Cross-modal scope**: Current vision experiment only tests ResNet-18. ViT would be architecturally distinct.

#### 5.2 Implementation Approach (to maintain current experiment design)
**Option A: Replace ResNet with ViT while keeping expert pool & router the same**
1. Load `torchvision.models.vision_transformer.vit_b_16(pretrained=True)` 
2. Extract patch embeddings before cls token aggregation (shape varies by ViT size)
3. Adapt feature extraction to handle sequence layout instead of spatial maps
4. Keep identical 5-expert pool (would need adaptation for 1D sequence vs 2D spatial)
5. Same 4 conditions (A/B/C/D with norm/frozen combinations)
6. Same CIFAR-10 dataset (ViT expects 224×224 or 384×384; requires resize preprocessing)

**Option B: Add ViT as a second backbone in parallel experiment**
1. Create new script `run_vision_moe_vit_collapse.py` (parallel to v2)
2. Abstract backbone interface: `ResNet18FeatureExtractor` → `BackboneInterface` with `.forward()` → feature tensors
3. Implement `ViTFeatureExtractor` subclass
4. Same MoE/router/conditions applied to ViT features

#### 5.3 Key Implementation Challenges
1. **Spatial topology mismatch**: ResNet (B,C,H,W) vs ViT (B,N,D) — expert pool expects spatial maps
   - LastPatchExpert assumes 2D indexing `feat_maps[:,:,-1,-1]`
   - AttentionPoolExpert assumes 2D spatial grid
   
2. **CIFAR-10 resize overhead**: ViT typically expects ≥224×224 input
   - CIFAR-10 is 32×32; upsampling adds compute & potential distribution shift
   - Alternative: Use ImageNet-32 or downsampled ImageNet subsets
   
3. **Normalization semantics**: InstanceNorm2d on (B,C,H,W) vs sequence-based normalization
   - May need LayerNorm for ViT features instead

4. **Feature dimension**: ViT-B has 768-dim hidden; ResNet-18 has 512-dim
   - Router gate & expert layers need reconfigured dimensions

#### 5.4 Deployment Readiness
- **RACE VM torch version**: ≥2.1.0 (supports native ViT)
- **No additional pip install needed** if using torchvision.models
- **Timm would require**: `pip install timm` in run_vision_moe_race.sh (line 13 pattern could extend)

### 6. COLLAPSE MECHANISM SUMMARY

**Time-series (where normalization CAUSES collapse):**
- Backbone: MOMENT/Chronos/etc. outputs normalized hidden states
- RevIN strips per-window mean/scale (the routing signal)
- Router becomes degenerate → gradient co-adaptation → routing collapse

**Vision with ResNet (where normalization PREVENTS collapse):**
- Backbone: ResNet outputs spatial feature maps
- InstanceNorm2d strips per-channel spatial statistics (NOT the routing signal)
- Routing signal is in spatial patterns → normalization doesn't harm routing
- WITHOUT normalization: unfrozen backbone co-adapts to dominant expert → collapse (Condition C)
- WITH normalization: spatial patterns preserved → no collapse (Conditions A, D)

**ViT cross-modal implication:**
- ViT processes patches as sequences (conceptually similar to time-series tokens)
- Key question: Does ViT's learned [CLS] token routing depend on per-patch statistics?
- Hypothesis: InstanceNorm on ViT patch embeddings might behave like RevIN (enable collapse), making it intermediate between ResNet (prevents) and TSFM (causes)

```
40	# ============================================================
41	
42	class ResNet18FeatureExtractor(nn.Module):
43	    """ResNet-18 that returns feature maps (B, 512, H, W) instead of logits."""
44	
45	    def __init__(self, pretrained=True):
46	        super().__init__()
47	        base = torchvision.models.resnet18(
48	            weights=torchvision.models.ResNet18_Weights.DEFAULT if pretrained else None
49	        )
50	        # Adapt for 32x32 CIFAR input
51	        base.conv1 = nn.Conv2d(3, 64, kernel_size=3, stride=1, padding=1, bias=False)
52	        base.maxpool = nn.Identity()
53	
54	        # Keep everything except avgpool and fc
55	        self.conv1 = base.conv1
56	        self.bn1 = base.bn1
57	        self.relu = base.relu
58	        self.layer1 = base.layer1
59	        self.layer2 = base.layer2
60	        self.layer3 = base.layer3
61	        self.layer4 = base.layer4
62	
63	    def forward(self, x):
64	        x = self.relu(self.bn1(self.conv1(x)))
65	        x = self.layer1(x)
66	        x = self.layer2(x)
67	        x = self.layer3(x)
68	        x = self.layer4(x)
69	        return x  # (B, 512, 4, 4) for CIFAR-10
```

```
135	
136	
137	# ============================================================
138	# Vision MoE with diverse experts
139	# ============================================================
140	
141	class VisionMoEv2(nn.Module):
142	    """MoE with diverse expert architectures and optional normalization on feature maps."""
143	
144	    def __init__(self, d_feat: int, num_classes: int, norm_type: str = "none"):
145	        super().__init__()
146	        self.norm_type = norm_type
147	
148	        # Normalization on feature maps BEFORE pooling (RevIN analog)
149	        if norm_type == "instancenorm":
150	            self.norm = nn.InstanceNorm2d(d_feat, affine=False)
151	        elif norm_type == "batchnorm":
152	            self.norm = nn.BatchNorm2d(d_feat)
153	        else:
154	            self.norm = None
155	
156	        # Router: pool normalized features → gate logits
157	        self.router_pool = nn.AdaptiveAvgPool2d(1)
158	        self.router_gate = nn.Linear(d_feat, 5)
159	
160	        # 5 architecturally distinct experts (matching time-series RR-MoA)
161	        self.experts = nn.ModuleList([
162	            MeanPoolExpert(d_feat, num_classes),
163	            MaxPoolExpert(d_feat, num_classes),
164	            AttentionPoolExpert(d_feat, num_classes),
165	            ConvPoolExpert(d_feat, num_classes),
166	            LastPatchExpert(d_feat, num_classes),
167	        ])
168	        self.K = len(self.experts)
169	
170	    def forward(self, feat_maps):
171	        """
172	        Args:
173	            feat_maps: (B, C, H, W) from backbone
174	        Returns:
175	            logits: (B, num_classes)
176	            routing_weights: (B, K)
177	            routing_entropy: scalar
178	        """
179	        # Apply normalization to feature maps (strips per-channel spatial statistics)
180	        if self.norm is not None:
181	            normed = self.norm(feat_maps)
182	        else:
183	            normed = feat_maps
184	
185	        # Route on pooled normalized features
186	        pooled = self.router_pool(normed).flatten(1)  # (B, C)
187	        gate_logits = self.router_gate(pooled)
188	        weights = F.softmax(gate_logits, dim=-1)  # (B, K)
189	
190	        # Experts operate on normalized features (same as AdaMix)
191	        expert_outputs = torch.stack(
192	            [exp(normed) for exp in self.experts], dim=1
193	        )  # (B, K, num_classes)
194	        logits = (weights.unsqueeze(-1) * expert_outputs).sum(dim=1)  # (B, num_classes)
195	
196	        # Aggregate entropy
197	        with torch.no_grad():
198	            avg_probs = weights.mean(dim=0)
199	            entropy = -(avg_probs * torch.log(avg_probs + 1e-10)).sum().item()
200	
201	        return logits, weights, entropy
202	
203	
204	# ============================================================
```

```
1360	
1361	To test whether normalization-induced routing collapse transfers beyond time series, we construct a controlled vision experiment: ResNet-18 (pretrained on ImageNet, adapted for CIFAR-10) as backbone, with $K{=}5$ architecturally diverse expert classifiers (mean-pool, max-pool, attention-pool, conv-pool, last-patch---matching the time-series expert pool), no load-balancing loss, and the same four conditions tested in the time-series experiments.
1362	
1363	\begin{table}[h]
1364	\centering
1365	\caption{\textbf{Vision MoE routing collapse control} (ResNet-18 + CIFAR-10, $K{=}5$ diverse experts, 20 epochs, mean$\pm$std over 3 seeds). Gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}) is confirmed: the unnormalized unfrozen backbone collapses (C). However, adding InstanceNorm2d or BatchNorm2d \emph{prevents} collapse (A, D)---confirming Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction that collapse requires the stripped statistics to carry routing signal.}
1366	\label{tab:vision_moe}

1369	\toprule
1370	Condition & Configuration & Entropy (final) & Collapses? & Test Acc \\
1371	\midrule
1372	A & Unfrozen + InstanceNorm2d & $1.475 \pm 0.021$ & No & $0.919$ \\
1373	B & Frozen + InstanceNorm2d   & $0.992 \pm 0.041$ & No & $0.577$ \\
1374	C & Unfrozen + No norm        & $\mathbf{0.184 \pm 0.260}$ & \textbf{Yes} & $0.921$ \\
1375	D & Unfrozen + BatchNorm2d    & $1.476 \pm 0.031$ & No & $0.919$ \\

1379	
1380	\textbf{Key finding: normalization prevents rather than causes collapse in vision.} Without normalization (C), the unfrozen backbone freely co-adapts with the dominant expert, collapsing routing entropy to $0.184\pm0.260$ (vs.\ max $1.609$; two of three seeds reach ${\leq}0.075$)---directly confirming the gradient co-adaptation mechanism of Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}. However, adding InstanceNorm2d (A) or BatchNorm2d (D) \emph{prevents} collapse (entropy $1.48\pm0.02$). This is the \emph{opposite} of time series, where normalization enables collapse.
1381	
1382	\textbf{Explanation via Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}.} In time series, per-window mean $M$ and scale $\Sigma$ are the primary routing signal ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$); RevIN strips exactly these statistics. In vision, routing-discriminative information resides in \emph{spatial feature patterns}, not in per-channel spatial statistics. InstanceNorm2d strips per-channel spatial mean and variance---which carry negligible routing information for image classification. This is analogous to Traffic (the time-series dataset with lowest routing signal ratio $R{=}0.14$, where RR-MoA provides no advantage). The collapse mechanism (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}) is modality-general; the normalization \emph{trigger} (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) depends on whether the stripped statistics overlap with the routing signal.
```

```
53	\begin{abstract}
54	We discover a fundamental incompatibility between instance normalization and mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing on time series foundation models (TSFMs): routing entropy drops to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, yet the failure is not entropy collapse---a normalization dose-response reveals that entropy stays high while MSE degrades $283\%$ (phase transition at full normalization). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: RevIN strips per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. Seven converging causal controls isolate this---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---and all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) predicts which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The diagnosis is precise enough that the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on raw pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones (Timer-XL $12/12$; Moirai-MoE $15/15$; $65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
55	\end{abstract}
56	
57	\section{Introduction}
58	\label{sec:intro}
59	
60	The transformer architecture~\citep{vaswani2017attention} has enabled foundation models~\citep{bommasani2021foundation} across language~\citep{devlin2019bert, brown2020gpt3} and vision~\citep{dosovitskiy2021vit}. Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer/Timer-XL~\citep{liu2024timer, liu2025timerxl}, Moirai/Moirai-MoE~\citep{woo2024moirai, liu2025moiraimoe}, UniTS~\citep{lee2024units}, Lag-Llama~\citep{rasul2024lagllama}, Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, and TTM~\citep{ekambaram2024ttm} have emerged as powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a concrete practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed and trained?}
61	
62	\textbf{The one-size-fits-all adapter status quo.} Current TSFM adaptation reduces to: \emph{pool hidden states $\to$ project to horizon}, with standard variants---linear probing~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm}, MLP heads~\citep{nie2023patchtst, wu2023timesnet}, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, and bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}. Every public TSFM benchmark uses a \emph{single static} adapter per dataset. Yet real time series are non-stationary: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology is unlikely optimal for every window. This motivates a \emph{mixture of adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct expert heads with a learned router assigning each window to the most appropriate expert.
63	
64	\textbf{Why frozen at all?} The frozen-backbone paradigm enables deployment regimes that per-dataset baselines like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with OTA adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
65	
66	\textbf{MoE on TSFMs collapses.} When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to a TSFM with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configs (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, reshaping the backbone to serve it better in a feedback loop within ${\sim}50$ steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even when frozen, hidden-state routing underperforms because RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the statistics the router needs. RevIN applied to the router degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}); Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} (no RevIN) does \emph{not} collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), confirming the failure is normalization-specific.
67	
68	\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while the backbone is kept strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The fix's simplicity is a direct consequence of the diagnosis's precision---the intervention is unambiguous once the cause is isolated, whereas 11 optimization-side mechanisms all fail (720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).
69	
70	Our contributions:
71	
72	\textbf{(1)~A general information-theoretic principle for MoE routing on any normalized backbone.} We prove a mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) showing that instance normalization destroys routing MI equal to the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. This yields a quantitative prediction---validated at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$) across nine datasets, including correctly predicting where raw routing does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}). The principle generalizes across all five tested backbones without retraining the predictor.
73	
74	\textbf{(2)~Seven converging causal controls via opposite-trajectory analysis.} The strongest evidence: under identical unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL (no RevIN) entropy \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$; Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast})---the same code, the same data, only the normalization differs. Six additional controls---RevIN ablation, Moirai/Chronos negative controls, RevIN-applied-to-router, entropy regularization, and a 720-run rescue sweep~\citep{fedus2022switch, zoph2022stmoe, zhang2025remoe, zhou2022expertchoice} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue})---all converge on the same diagnosis.
75	
76	\textbf{(3)~RR-MoA and the Frozen Paradox.} RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$, Wilcoxon; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), outperforming LoRA, TRACE, ensembles, and full fine-tuning. Frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$---the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}. RR-MoA generalizes across four backbones with instance normalization (MOMENT-small/large, Moirai, Moirai-MoE); two negative controls without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm normalization-specificity. Dual-stream experts close the DLinear gap from $+57\%$ to $+5\%$, \textbf{beating} DLinear on Weather ($0.198$ vs $0.208$, $p{=}0.03$; \S\ref{sec:main_results}).
77	
78	\section{Related Work}
79	
80	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} Standard adaptation uses LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} or bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters} with manually chosen configurations. MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning for encoder-based TSFMs. TRACE~\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads---the closest work, but heads are hand-designed. Beyond LoRA~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos. AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations. Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning. In-context fine-tuning~\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} addresses model selection; GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval} provides benchmarking. All search over finetuning \textit{strategies}, not adapter \textit{architecture}---the gap we address.
81	
82	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been applied within TSFM backbones: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} scales to billions of parameters via sparse activation. These apply MoE \emph{within} the backbone architecture; segment-wise approaches further specialize experts by temporal segments. In contrast, RR-MoA applies MoE at the \emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal and complementary design axis, which we empirically validate by evaluating RR-MoA \emph{on} Moirai-MoE (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}): RR-MoA still provides a $-37\%$ MSE improvement on a backbone that already contains internal expert routing, confirming the two design axes stack.
83	
84	\textbf{MoE routing collapse.} A large body of work addresses routing collapse via \emph{optimization-side} interventions: Switch Transformer load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReMoE ReLU gates~\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} introduces adapter mixtures for NLP but routes on hidden states. All enforce diversity via optimization constraints without addressing whether the router's input contains discriminative information. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we identify when routing information is structurally absent from hidden states, and propose moving the router's input out of the normalized representation as the minimal causal fix.
85	
86	\textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics---both recognizing that instance normalization discards predictive information. We identify a previously unknown consequence: RevIN destroys the per-window statistics that MoE routers require for discriminative gating. RR-MoA operationalizes the ``statistic re-injection'' insight specifically for routing.
87	
```

  "plan": "# Plan: Tighten Proposition 2 — Exact MI Loss Measurement + Bound Tightness\n\n## Context\n\n**Problem:** Proposition 2's lower bound (Part ii) is empirically **vacuous on all 9 datasets** — `max(I(M,Sigma;E) - epsilon, 0) = 0.0` everywhere because the KSG-estimated leakage epsilon exceeds the k-NN-estimated I(M,Sigma;E) on every dataset. A theory-focused NeurIPS reviewer will notice this immediately. The bound tells the reviewer nothing, yet the paper claims it \"is informative when I(M,Sigma;E) >> epsilon.\"\n\n**Root causes:**\n1. Estimation inconsistency: ε estimated via CCA+KSG (upward-biased on small N), I(M,Σ;E) via k-NN conditional entropy (different bias profile)\n2. Router collapse during MI estimation: `train_router_only()` in `compute_direct_mi.py` produces severely imbalanced experts (Traffic: 10000/10000 in one expert; ETTm2: 7250/7567 in expert 0), making I(M,Σ;E) ≈ 0 trivially\n3. The bound IS genuinely loose — the gap I(M,Σ;S|E) is non-negligible\n\n**Strategy:** Bypass the bound with **direct exact MI loss measurement** via H(E|S), fix the router collapse in estimation, and add synthetic ground-truth validation.\n\n---\n\n## Tier 1: Exact MI Loss Measurement (Must-do, ~3-4 days)\n\n### Step 1.1: Create `scripts/compute_exact_mi_loss.py`\n\n**What it computes per dataset:**\n- Train a Conv1d router on raw X with **stronger entropy regularization** (coef=1.0, up from 0.5) to ensure balanced expert assignments for cleaner MI analysis\n- Extract expert assignments E\n- Compute **H(E)** from empirical expert distribution (trivial)\n- Train MLP classifier to predict E from shape S → validation cross-entropy estimates **H(E|S)**\n- Compute **exact MI loss** = H(E|S) = H(E) - I(S;E)\n- Compute **entropy retention** = I(S;E) / H(E) = 1 - H(E|S)/H(E) — the fraction of routing information surviving normalization\n- Compute **within-expert I(M,Σ; S|E)** via per-expert CCA+KSG decomposition (the theoretical gap)\n- Consistency check: H(E|S) ≈ [I(M,Σ;E) - ε] + I(M,Σ;S|E)\n\n**Key design decisions:**\n- **MLP classifier architecture:** Linear(512,256) → GELU → Dropout(0.1) → Linear(256,128) → GELU → Linear(128,K). 80/20 stratified split, 50 epochs, Adam lr=1e-3, early stopping patience=10 on val CE. Temperature scaling post-hoc.\n- **Router entropy reg = 1.0** (not 0.5) to ensure minimum expert usage ≥ 5% for valid MI estimation. Skip within-expert CCA for experts with N < 50 windows.\n- **3 router seeds** {42,43,44} per dataset to quantify stochasticity. Report mean ± std.\n\n**Reuse from existing code:**\n- `load_channel_standardized_windows()` from `scripts/analyze_routing_signal_ratio.py`\n- `build_router()`, `train_router_only()` from `scripts/compute_direct_mi.py` (modify entropy coef)\n- CCA+KSG pipeline from `scripts/compute_ksg_leakage.py:compute_leakage()`\n\n**Output:** `results/analysis/exact_mi_loss.json`\n```json\n{\n  \"ETTh1\": {\n    \"H_E\": 1.45, \"H_E_given_S\": 0.89, \"I_S_E\": 0.56,\n    \"exact_mi_loss\": 0.89, \"retention\": 0.386,\n    \"I_MS_E\": 0.52, \"epsilon\": 0.45, \"lower_bound\": 0.07,\n    \"gap_I_MS_S_given_E\": 0.82, \"gap_ratio_logK\": 0.51,\n    \"classifier_accuracy\": 0.42, \"expert_balance\": [0.18, 0.22, ...]\n  }, ...\n}\n```\n\n**Runs on:** RACE VM GPU (~20 min/dataset × 3 seeds × 9 datasets ≈ 9 hours). Can parallelize across seeds.\n\n### Step 1.2: Create `scripts/plot_mi_tightness.py`\n\n**Two figures for the paper:**\n- **Left panel:** Scatter of H(E|S) [exact MI loss] vs Δ% across 9 datasets. If this correlates well (better than ρ=-0.47 from direct MI), it's a strong result.\n- **Right panel:** Stacked bar per dataset showing: lower bound (blue) + gap (red) = exact (total height). Visualizes where the bound is tight vs vacuous.\n\n**Runs on:** Local CPU, reads `exact_mi_loss.json`.\n\n### Step 1.3: Also recompute I(M,Σ;E) with consistent estimator\n\nThe current `compute_direct_mi.py` uses k-NN conditional entropy (K_nn=20) which has known downward bias for small mutual information. ## Approved Plan:
# Plan: Tighten Proposition 2 — Exact MI Loss Measurement + Bound Tightness

## Context

**Problem:** Proposition 2's lower bound (Part ii) is empirically **vacuous on all 9 datasets** — `max(I(M,Sigma;E) - epsilon, 0) = 0.0` everywhere because the KSG-estimated leakage epsilon exceeds the k-NN-estimated I(M,Sigma;E) on every dataset. A theory-focused NeurIPS reviewer will notice this immediately. The bound tells the reviewer nothing, yet the paper claims it "is informative when I(M,Sigma;E) >> epsilon."

**Root causes:**
1. Estimation inconsistency: ε estimated via CCA+KSG (upward-biased on small N), I(M,Σ;E) via k-NN conditional entropy (different bias profile)
2. Router collapse during MI estimation: `train_router_only()` in `compute_direct_mi.py` produces severely imbalanced experts (Traffic: 10000/10000 in one expert; ETTm2: 7250/7567 in expert 0), making I(M,Σ;E) ≈ 0 trivially
3. The bound IS genuinely loose — the gap I(M,Σ;S|E) is non-negligible

**Strategy:** Bypass the bound with **direct exact MI loss measurement** via H(E|S), fix the router collapse in estimation, and add synthetic ground-truth validation.

---

## Tier 1: Exact MI Loss Measurement (Must-do, ~3-4 days)

### Step 1.1: Create `scripts/compute_exact_mi_loss.py`

**What it computes per dataset:**
- Train a Conv1d router on raw X with **stronger entropy regularization** (coef=1.0, up from 0.5) to ensure balanced expert assignments for cleaner MI analysis
- Extract expert assignments E
- Compute **H(E)** from empirical expert distribution (trivial)
- Train MLP classifier to predict E from shape S → validation cross-entropy estimates **H(E|S)**
- Compute **exact MI loss** = H(E|S) = H(E) - I(S;E)
- Compute **entropy retention** = I(S;E) / H(E) = 1 - H(E|S)/H(E) — the fraction of routing information surviving normalization
- Compute **within-expert I(M,Σ; S|E)** via per-expert CCA+KSG decomposition (the theoretical gap)
- Consistency check: H(E|S) ≈ [I(M,Σ;E) - ε] + I(M,Σ;S|E)

**Key design decisions:**
- **MLP classifier architecture:** Linear(512,256) → GELU → Dropout(0.1) → Linear(256,128) → GELU → Linear(128,K). 80/20 stratified split, 50 epochs, Adam lr=1e-3, early stopping patience=10 on val CE. Temperature scaling post-hoc.
- **Router entropy reg = 1.0** (not 0.5) to ensure minimum expert usage ≥ 5% for valid MI estimation. Skip within-expert CCA for experts with N < 50 windows.
- **3 router seeds** {42,43,44} per dataset to quantify stochasticity. Report mean ± std.

**Reuse from existing code:**
- `load_channel_standardized_windows()` from `scripts/analyze_routing_signal_ratio.py`
- `build_router()`, `train_router_only()` from `scripts/compute_direct_mi.py` (modify entropy coef)
- CCA+KSG pipeline from `scripts/compute_ksg_leakage.py:compute_leakage()`

**Output:** `results/analysis/exact_mi_loss.json`
```json
{
  "ETTh1": {
    "H_E": 1.45, "H_E_given_S": 0.89, "I_S_E": 0.56,
    "exact_mi_loss": 0.89, "retention": 0.386,
    "I_MS_E": 0.52, "epsilon": 0.45, "lower_bound": 0.07,
    "gap_I_MS_S_given_E": 0.82, "gap_ratio_logK": 0.51,
    "classifier_accuracy": 0.42, "expert_balance": [0.18, 0.22, ...]
  }, ...
}
```

**Runs on:** RACE VM GPU (~20 min/dataset × 3 seeds × 9 datasets ≈ 9 hours). Can parallelize across seeds.

### Step 1.2: Create `scripts/plot_mi_tightness.py`

**Two figures for the paper:**
- **Left panel:** Scatter of H(E|S) [exact MI loss] vs Δ% across 9 datasets. If this correlates well (better than ρ=-0.47 from direct MI), it's a strong result.
- **Right panel:** Stacked bar per dataset showing: lower bound (blue) + gap (red) = exact (total height). Visualizes where the bound is tight vs vacuous.

**Runs on:** Local CPU, reads `exact_mi_loss.json`.

### Step 1.3: Also recompute I(M,Σ;E) with consistent estimator

The current `compute_direct_mi.py` uses k-NN conditional entropy (K_nn=20) which has known downward bias for small mutual information. Recompute with the same CCA+KSG method used for ε, ensuring estimation consistency. This may rescue the bound from being vacuous on some datasets.

---

## Tier 2: Synthetic Ground-Truth Validation (~2-3 days)

### Step 2.1: Create `scripts/synthetic_mi_tightness.py`

**Design:** K=5 Gaussian clusters with controlled (M,Σ) ↔ S correlation.
- Cluster k: mean μ_k, scale σ_k drawn from known distribution
- Within cluster: X ~ N(μ_k, σ_k² · Σ_within) where Σ_within has off-diagonal ρ_MS controlling shape-statistics correlation
- Sweep ρ_MS ∈ {0.0, 0.1, ..., 0.9} × 5 seeds

**All MI quantities computed analytically** (Gaussian closed forms) AND via the estimation pipeline. Plot:
- x: ρ_MS, y: exact loss and lower bound (both ground-truth and estimated)
- Shows bound degradation and estimation accuracy simultaneously

**Key output:** "Estimation error < X% relative to ground truth; bound tight within Y nats at ρ_MS < Z"

**Runs on:** CPU only, ~30 min total.

---

## Tier 3: Paper Integration (~1-2 days)

### Step 3.1: Revise Proposition 2 in `main.tex`

**Option A (preferred): Add Part (v) to Proposition 2:**
> **(v) Exact loss and tightness.** Direct measurement via held-out classifier cross-entropy gives H(E|S) = X–Y nats across nine datasets, corresponding to Z–W% entropy retention. The lower bound (ii) is tight within Q nats on P/9 datasets. The gap I(M,Σ; S|E) is largest on datasets with strong trend-seasonality coupling.

**Option B: Reframe Part (iv) around the exact measurement**, keeping the bound as explanatory theory and leading with the direct number.

### Step 3.2: New appendix table (`tab:mi_tightness`)

| ETTh1   | ...   | ...     | ...       | ... | ... | ...      |

### Step 3.3: Revise the footnote at line 475

Currently: "Direct MI estimation via KNN yields ρ=-0.47; R(D) outperforms it because the variance proxy is robust to the discrete structure"

Update: Report the H(E|S) vs Δ% correlation (expected to be much stronger than ρ=-0.47 because H(E|S) is the exact quantity, not a noisy MI estimate).

### Step 3.4: Update `evidence_vm/verify.py`

Add checks for:
- H(E|S) values match JSON ↔ main.tex
- Bound non-negativity: gap ≥ 0 for all datasets
- Consistency: H(E|S) ≈ (I(M,Σ;E) - ε) + I(M,Σ;S|E) within tolerance
- Retention percentages match claimed values

### Step 3.5: Revise limitations (line 562)

Remove or soften "R(D) uses only first moments" limitation — the exact H(E|S) measurement doesn't have this limitation. Keep only the Solar outlier note.

```
204	# ============================================================
205	# Data
206	# ============================================================
207	
208	def get_cifar10(batch_size=128, data_dir="./data"):
209	    transform_train = transforms.Compose([
210	        transforms.RandomCrop(32, padding=4),
211	        transforms.RandomHorizontalFlip(),
212	        transforms.ToTensor(),
213	        transforms.Normalize((0.4914, 0.4822, 0.4465), (0.2023, 0.1994, 0.2010)),
214	    ])
215	    transform_test = transforms.Compose([
216	        transforms.ToTensor(),
217	        transforms.Normalize((0.4914, 0.4822, 0.4465), (0.2023, 0.1994, 0.2010)),
218	    ])
219	
220	    train_set = torchvision.datasets.CIFAR10(
221	        root=data_dir, train=True, download=True, transform=transform_train
222	    )
223	    test_set = torchvision.datasets.CIFAR10(
224	        root=data_dir, train=False, download=True, transform=transform_test
225	    )
226	
227	    train_loader = torch.utils.data.DataLoader(
228	        train_set, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=True, num_workers=2, pin_memory=True
229	    )
230	    test_loader = torch.utils.data.DataLoader(
231	        test_set, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=False, num_workers=2, pin_memory=True
232	    )
233	    return train_loader, test_loader
234	
235	
236	# ============================================================
237	# Training
238	# ============================================================
239	
240	def train_condition(backbone, moe, train_loader, test_loader, epochs, device, frozen, lr=1e-3):
241	    """Train one condition. No load-balancing loss — let routing specialize naturally."""
242	    backbone.to(device)
243	    moe.to(device)
244	
245	    if frozen:
246	        backbone.eval()
247	        for p in backbone.parameters():
248	            p.requires_grad = False
249	        params = list(moe.parameters())
250	    else:
251	        backbone.train()
252	        params = list(backbone.parameters()) + list(moe.parameters())
253	
254	    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(params, lr=lr)
255	    criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()
256	
257	    trajectory = []
258	    global_step = 0
259	
260	    for epoch in range(epochs):
261	        if not frozen:
262	            backbone.train()
263	        moe.train()
264	
265	        for images, labels in train_loader:
266	            images, labels = images.to(device), labels.to(device)
267	
268	            # Forward
269	            if frozen:
270	                with torch.no_grad():
271	                    feat_maps = backbone(images)
272	            else:
273	                feat_maps = backbone(images)
274	
275	            logits, weights, entropy = moe(feat_maps)
276	            loss = criterion(logits, labels)
277	
278	            # Backward
279	            optimizer.zero_grad()
280	            loss.backward()
281	
282	            # Log gradient norms
283	            with torch.no_grad():
284	                expert_grad_norms = []
285	                for exp in moe.experts:
286	                    gnorm = sum(
287	                        p.grad.norm().item() ** 2
288	                        for p in exp.parameters() if p.grad is not None
289	                    ) ** 0.5
290	                    expert_grad_norms.append(round(gnorm, 6))
291	
292	                router_grad_norm = sum(
293	                    p.grad.norm().item() ** 2
294	                    for p in moe.router_gate.parameters() if p.grad is not None
295	                ) ** 0.5
296	
297	                if not frozen:
298	                    backbone_grad_norm = sum(
299	                        p.grad.norm().item() ** 2
300	                        for p in backbone.parameters() if p.grad is not None
301	                    ) ** 0.5
302	                else:
303	                    backbone_grad_norm = 0.0
304	
305	                preds = logits.argmax(dim=-1)
306	                acc = (preds == labels).float().mean().item()
307	
308	                # Per-expert usage (fraction of samples where each expert has max weight)
309	                max_expert = weights.argmax(dim=-1)
310	                usage = [(max_expert == k).float().mean().item() for k in range(moe.K)]
311	
312	            optimizer.step()
313	
314	            trajectory.append({
315	                "step": global_step,
316	                "epoch": epoch,
317	                "loss": round(loss.item(), 6),
318	                "routing_entropy": round(entropy, 6),
319	                "routing_max_weight": round(weights.mean(dim=0).max().item(), 6),
320	                "mean_routing_weights": [round(w, 6) for w in weights.mean(dim=0).tolist()],
321	                "expert_usage": [round(u, 4) for u in usage],
322	                "expert_grad_norms": expert_grad_norms,
323	                "router_grad_norm": round(router_grad_norm, 6),
324	                "backbone_grad_norm": round(backbone_grad_norm, 6),
325	                "train_acc": round(acc, 4),
326	            })
327	            global_step += 1
328	
329	    # Test evaluation
330	    backbone.eval()
331	    moe.eval()
332	    correct = 0
333	    total = 0
334	    test_entropies = []
335	
336	    with torch.no_grad():
337	        for images, labels in test_loader:
338	            images, labels = images.to(device), labels.to(device)
339	            feat_maps = backbone(images)
340	            logits, weights, entropy = moe(feat_maps)
341	            correct += (logits.argmax(dim=-1) == labels).sum().item()
342	            total += labels.size(0)
343	            test_entropies.append(entropy)
344	
345	    test_acc = correct / total
346	    test_entropy = np.mean(test_entropies)
347	
348	    return trajectory, test_acc, test_entropy
```
